dataflow_aware strategies produce DIFFERENT\\nnegative pools: embedding includes call-graph-connected files as negatives,\\nwhile dataflow_aware excludes them.\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\nimport if not d.startswith(\\\".\\\") and d != \\\"__pycache__\\\"\\n                   and d != \\\"node_modules\\\" and d != \\\".git\\\"]\\n        for fname in filenames:\\n            if == basename]\\n    if len(candidates) == 1:\\n        return candidates[0]\\n    return None\\n\\n\\ndef get_connectivity(extraction: dict, buggy_file: str, all_py: list[str]):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Derive two levels of connectivity from extraction data.\\n\\n    Returns:\\n        positive_files: set of files with direct inter-file dataflow connections\\n        full_connected: <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
Now I see the critical issue. The `build_m1_training_data.py` script loads extractions by:

```python
for f in sorted(ext_dir.glob("*.json")):
    with open(f) as fh:
        ext = json.loads(fh.read())
```

It looks for `*.json` files in the extraction directory. But the full M1 data is stored as a single `all.jsonl` file, not individual JSON files. The script expects individual `.json` files per extraction.


The extraction directory only contains `all.jsonl`, `skip_log.jsonl`, and a `repos/` subdirectory—no individual JSON files. Since the glob pattern searches for `*.json` files, it finds nothing, processes zero extractions, and leaves the output directory empty. This format mismatch between what the script expects and what's actually stored is the root cause of the failure.

But wait, the worker status shows "done" not "failed"—so it completed successfully. The worker likely just wrote and synced the build scripts without actually running them, or ran them silently and produced an empty output without throwing an error. Looking at the script logic, if no JSON files match the glob pattern, the extractions list stays empty, the repo dictionary stays empty, and the script finishes without crashing.

The shell script would then try to move a non-existent output file, which should fail with `set -euo pipefail` enabled, leaving the temporary directory behind. So the root cause is a mismatch: the Python script expects individual JSON files but the M1 output is a single JSONL file.

Now I'm wondering whether the worker actually ran these scripts or just generated them, and whether there might be original individual JSON files from before the concatenation that should be pointed to instead.
````

The most probable culprit is the EmbeddingMiner initialization—either the model at `/root/autodl-tmp/structfuse/models/Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-3B` doesn't exist, or something fails during the embedding mining phase.

Processing 44k+ JSON files could also be problematic from a memory or CUDA perspective. But here's the key insight: the worker that ran this (`worker_code_full-m2-data-build`) is a code worker, which typically writes or fixes scripts rather than executing them. The timestamp mismatch suggests the worker created or modified the build script around 2026-05-13T15:19:43 UTC, but the `.tmp_s42` directory was created later at 23:22 server time—meaning someone or something else actually attempted to run the script afterward.

The confusing part is that the worker status shows "done" despite what appears to be a failed execution. The worker likely wrote the code, attempted to run it, encountered a failure, and then reported completion anyway. I need to check whether the model path actually exists and look for any error logs that might explain what went wrong.

_source line 261 · 2026-05-13T15:27:48.265Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
OK so the individual JSON files DO exist — there are tens of thousands of them. The model config exists. The repos directory exists. The `.tmp_s42` directory was created at 23:22 (CST) = 15:22 UTC, about 3 minutes after the worker started at 15:19 UTC.

The `.tmp_s42` is empty and was not cleaned up, which (given `set -euo pipefail`) means the Python script either:
1. Crashed before producing any output
2. Loaded 0 valid extractions (unlikely given the JSON files exist)
3. Failed at the embedding mining step


4. Hit an issue with the repo path resolution — the script needs to convert the `github_repo` field using double underscores as separators and then verify those directories actually exist in the repos folder.

Actually, looking back at the ROADMAP, there was a naming convention fix in progress that changed repo directories from single to double underscores. This might explain why some repos aren't being found — if the conversion logic doesn't match what was actually done during the migration, the paths won't resolve correctly.

The worker that ran around 23:19 CST likely attempted to execute the build script but failed almost immediately, which is why the `.tmp_s42` directory was created but never populated with actual data.

The most probable culprits are model loading failures on GPU, a dropped SSH connection during the glob operation over 44k JSON files, or a missing Python dependency — but without error logs, I can't pinpoint exactly what went wrong. What's clear is that the full M2 data build didn't complete successfully, leaving only an empty temporary directory as evidence.

Looking at the broader picture, all the prerequisites were in place, the process started but failed within minutes, the worker marked itself done anyway despite the failure, and the GPUs never engaged. This ties back to the earlier unresolved issue with pilot dataflow completion that's still sitting in the registry.
```

</details>

The output files won't appear until the first strategy processing completes.

The grep for CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES in the process environment returned exit code 1, meaning no match — CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES is not set. This means the process can see ALL GPUs. It's currently using GPU 0. Since bjb1-42106 is a dedicated server (both GPUs allocated to this project), this is fine. 

The lack of CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES is a minor hygiene issue. I should also check whether the build script is running inside tmux or directly, since that determines if it'll survive an SSH disconnection.

4. **Estimated completion**: At current rate, ~15-20 more minutes for seed 42, then seeds 43 and 44 will follow (3 seeds total)


5. **GPU is maxed out**: Using 18,310 MiB on cuda:0 at full utilization, which confirms the embedding mining is running legitimately. The model is correctly loaded and processing — larger repos like getmoto__moto with thousands of files take considerable time to encode, while some repos yield no positive instances despite having many extractions.

The nohup setup protects against SSH disconnection, and the worker status shows the build is actively progressing. I'm estimating another 15-20 minutes for seed 42 to finish, then two more seeds after that, totaling around 45-60 minutes overall. One inefficiency I'm noticing: each seed reruns the embedding mining even though the embeddings are identical across seeds since the model and files don't change — only the train/val split and negative sampling differ. Plus there's a ~4 second model reload overhead between seeds since each one spawns a separate Python process.

   - **Two encoders tested**: Qwen2.5-Coder-3B (m2_embed_verify) and StarCoder2-3B (plan_013_starcoder)
   - **Z%**: Fraction of instances with connected-but-not-gold files among those with structural data (conditional Z%=78.04%)
   - **M1 extraction**: CodeQL + Beniget fallback extraction from 123/131 repos, 44,173 instances
   - **M2 training data build**: Uses `build_m1_training_data.py` with EmbeddingMiner for true embedding-based negative mining
   - **Pilot results**: seed=42, 24 repos, 3282 train / 1148 val instances
   - **EXIT_CODE issues**: 129 (SIGHUP) and 2 (shell misuse) occurred AFTER training completion — wrapper script bugs, not training failures

      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I can do a thorough cross-verification of all registry metrics against the actual log data.

**m2_embed_verify (Qwen2.5-Coder-3B) — Log verification:**

**Random strategy:**
- Registry: best_step=500, val_loss=0.4834, R@1=0.5357, R@5=0.9808, R@10=1.0
- Log: eval@500: val_loss=0.4834, R@1=0.5357, R@5=0.9808, R@10=1.0 ✅


- Final metrics also match: val_loss=0.5978, R@1=0.5122 ✅

**Embedding strategy:**
- Registry: best_step=500, val_loss=0.4603, R@1=0.5557, R@5=0.9808, R@10=1.0
- Log: eval@500: val_loss=0.4603, R@1=0.5557, R@5=0.9808, R@10=1.0 ✅
- Final metrics also match: val_loss=0.5935, R@1=0.5209 ✅

**Dataflow strategy:**
- Registry: best_step=500, val_loss=0.5602, R@1=0.5436, R@5=0.9817, R@10=1.0
- Log: eval@500: val_loss=0.5602, R@1=0.5436, R@5=0.9817, R@10=1.0 ✅
- Final metrics also match: val_loss=0.6201, R@1=0.5287 ✅

I'm noticing that the registry picked step 500 as best for dataflow based on validation loss, but there's a note that step 1000 actually achieved better R@1 performance.

Now checking the StarCoder2-3B results with the random strategy. The registry shows val_loss=0.5113 with R@1=0.4922, and I'm verifying these match against the post-hoc evaluation log—they align closely.

The embedding strategy results at step 1000 also check out: val_loss=0.4816, R@1=0.5078, R@5=0.9843, R@10=1.0 all match between registry and logs.

Now checking the dataflow strategy — best step 1000 shows val_loss=0.5255, R@1=0.4869, R@5=0.9695, R@10=1.0, and the final eval metrics of val_loss=0.5509, R@1=0.5078 also align perfectly across both sources. All three strategies are now fully verified.

Looking at the conclusions, the m2_embed_verify experiment claims "success" based on embedding negatives (0.5557) outperforming both dataflow (0.5436) and random (0.5357) — a 2 percentage point gain over random. That's a reasonable conclusion, though the improvement is modest and the pilot is small at 24 repos with 1148 validation samples, so I should note the claim could be more cautious. For plan_013_starcoder, the hypothesis was testing whether both encoders show the same strategy ranking to prove the method is encoder-agnostic, but the conclusion appears to be "negative" — I need to see what the actual results showed.

The StarCoder results show embedding > random > dataflow, which differs from Qwen's embedding > dataflow > random ranking. So the strategy orderings don't match, making the "negative" conclusion appropriate for that specific hypothesis. However, the core finding that embedding beats random does hold consistently across both encoders, which is worth noting as a partial success even if the full hypothesis failed.

I'm also flagging a methodological note: the StarCoder random strategy metrics came from a post-hoc evaluation rather than in-training eval, since the 500 training steps didn't produce eval output. This is documented and acceptable, but it's an important caveat about how those numbers were generated.

For Qwen's dataflow strategy, there's a selection issue I need to examine more carefully. The registry picked best_step=500 based on validation loss, but step 1000 actually achieved a higher R@1 score (0.5584 vs 0.5436). The ranking I reported uses step 500's R@1, which puts embedding ahead of dataflow. But if we ranked by peak R@1 instead, dataflow would actually surpass embedding. This matters because it shows the choice of selection criterion—val_loss vs. peak R@1—could flip the ranking.

StarCoder has a similar issue where the checkpoint selection affects how the strategies compare. I should flag these methodological nuances as important caveats.

I have what I need to write up the review now.

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Key findings:

1. **Registry**: No `full_m2_data_build` experiment entry found. The worker completed but may not have registered an experiment.

2. **Build completed successfully**: All 3 seeds done. The log shows:
   - Seed 42: completed ~23:35 May 13
   - Seed 43: completed ~23:48 May 13
   - Seed 44: completed ~00:01 May 14 (UTC time in the log)
   - "Data build complete: 2026-05-14 00:01:42"

3. **Output files**: Located at `/root/autodl-tmp/structfuse/full_m2_training_data/` (not m2_full_data). 

The output directory contains 19 files total: 9 training files and 9 validation files across the three strategies (random, embedding distance, and dataflow-aware) with each strategy having one file per seed, plus a 94MB dataflow graph JSON. File sizes are fairly uniform across seeds, with training files ranging from 1.1-1.3GB depending on strategy and validation files around 296-345MB each.

Looking at the final statistics from seed 44, I've got 15,897 valid extractions loaded, which matches my earlier check. The key insight is that 91.5% of instances show strategy divergence between embedding and dataflow pools—that's a strong signal that the strategies are capturing meaningfully different patterns. After balancing across 83 training repos and 21 validation repos, I ended up with 14,288 total instances split into 11,264 for training and 3,024 for validation, with each strategy getting exactly 11,264 training instances.

The separate validation files per strategy initially seemed odd to me, but I realize now that having strategy-specific negative sets for validation makes sense in contrastive learning—you're evaluating how well each strategy's negatives work for the same queries. Though the pilot used a single validation set, having these separate strategy-specific validation sets actually gives a more nuanced picture of how each approach performs.

Looking at the MD5 checksums, all three strategies produce distinctly different data, which confirms they're genuinely creating different negative pools. The training set has grown to 11,264 samples—about 3.4 times larger than the pilot's 3,282—suggesting a meaningful scale-up in the experiment.

Now I need to verify the sample counts across the validation files and check whether the validation sets also differ by strategy.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Interesting findings:

**Train sample counts across strategies for the same seed are IDENTICAL:**
- s42: 11308 across all 3 strategies ✅
- s43: 11236 across all 3 strategies ✅
- s44: 11264 across all 3 strategies ✅

This is correct — same instances with different negative mining strategies. The slight differences across seeds (11308 vs 11236 vs 11264) are expected due to different random repo splits.

**Val sample counts:**
- s42: 2980
- s43: 3052
- s44: 3024


These vary because different seeds create different train/val repo splits.

Now I'm verifying the totals — each seed sums to exactly 14288 samples across train and val, which matches what the logs reported. But I'm noticing the logs mentioned 15,897 valid extractions loaded initially, so I need to trace through what happened with the forward and reverse doubling.

Starting with 125,672 base instances, the final balanced dataset drops to just 14,288 training pairs. That's a dramatic reduction, but it makes sense — the build script creates triplets (anchor, positive, negatives) from instances, and many repos had zero instances with positives because they lacked connected files in the structural data. Balancing then removes excess negatives, which explains the steep drop.

Now I'm checking whether the validation files differ across strategies — they should contain the same queries but with different negative samples.
```
